## F1: Derivation 2 "Indistinguishable is identical"

The claim (lines 559-565): "Two candidates $\mathcal E,\mathcal E'$ for the same $p$ that both satisfy (F1), (F2), and (A) on $C$ are one account at grain $C$: their components are pairwise of one kind on $C$ and their answer profiles coincide."

Proof: "Immediate from Derivation 1 and (A)."

Derivation 1: If a transport satisfies (F1) on $C$, then every active component $k$ of $E$ has the same signature on $\tau[C]$ as its anchor $\lambda(k)$ has on $C$, up to the port translation.

So Derivation 1 says: for each component $k$ of $E$, sig of $k$ = sig of $\lambda(k)$ (anchor in $D$).

For two candidates $\mathcal E, \mathcal E'$ both satisfying (F1), each component of $E$ has the signature of its anchor in $D$, and each component of $E'$ has the signature of its anchor in $D$. But if the two candidates have different anchorings (different components of $D$ assigned to their components), then the components need not pair up.

Counter-instance 1: $D$ has ports $x,y,z$ with components $j_x: x=u$, $j_y: y=2x$, $j_z: z=y+1$. Candidate $E_1$ is $D$ itself. Candidate $E_2$ has ports $x,z$ with components $m_x: x=u$ (anchored to $\{j_x\}$) and $m_z: z=2x+1$ (anchored to $\{j_y,j_z\}$).

Let me check the claim's assumptions: both satisfy (F1), (F2), (A) on $C$.

For $E_2$:
- (F1) for $m_z$: $\operatorname{proj}_{x,z}\operatorname{Sol}_{\{j_y,j_z\}}(a,b_0)$. Under $\operatorname{do}(x=c)$, which replaces $j_x$ (not in the anchor), the solutions of the subnetwork $\{j_y,j_z\}$... hmm wait. $\operatorname{Sol}_{\lambda(k)}(a,b)$ — what does it mean to evaluate a subnetwork under an edit? The edit $\operatorname{do}(x=c)$ replaces the component assigning $x$, which is $j_x$. But $j_x$ is not in $\lambda(m_z) = \{j_y, j_z\}$. Hmm, but then how does the subnetwork's solution set depend on the edit? Let me think.

Actually, the subnetwork $\{j_y, j_z\}$ has ports $x, y, z$ where $x$ is an input to it (boundary of the subnetwork). When we evaluate $\operatorname{Sol}_{\lambda(k)}(a,b)$, presumably the edit acts on the whole organization and then we restrict to the subnetwork's components. Under $\operatorname{do}(x=c)$, $j_x$ is replaced, so $x$ is set to $c$... but wait, in the subnetwork $\{j_y, j_z\}$, the component $j_x$ is not present. So what determines $x$?

Hmm. Let's think about $\operatorname{proj}_{V_k}[\operatorname{Sol}_{\lambda(k)}(a,b)]$. $V_{m_z} = \{x, z\}$ presumably (the footprint of $m_z$ is $\{x,z\}$). And $\lambda(m_z) = \{j_y, j_z\}$ with a port translation — but the port translation maps... hmm.

Hmm wait, but there's a subtlety: under $\operatorname{do}(x=c)$, the value of $x$ is fixed to $c$ in the full solution. Actually here's a question: when we evaluate $\operatorname{Sol}_{\lambda(k)}(a,b)$, do we evaluate the subnetwork with the edit applied to it (i.e., the edit's effect on the subnetwork), or do we evaluate the subnetwork in the context of the edited whole? The formula says "the relation obtained by imposing the constraints of $\lambda(k)$ and projecting away its hidden ports". So we take the constraints of $\lambda(k)$ — that is, the relations $L_j(a,b)$ for $j\in\lambda(k)$ — and solve them, projecting away hidden ports. Under edit $\operatorname{do}(x=c)$, the relation $L_j$ for each $j\in\lambda(k)$ is evaluated at $(a,b)$. Since the edit replaces $j_x\notin\lambda(k)$, the relations of $j_y, j_z$ are unchanged. The finding's counter-instance: two candidates with different numbers of components (or same count but different relations). Both satisfy (F1), (F2), (A). But their components cannot be "pairwise of one kind" because there's no bijection (different counts) or because relations differ.

A correspondence can be *selected* — produced by blind variation and survival on a history of predictions — or *constructed* — produced by an episode of conjecture and criticism — or merely *declared* by whoever writes the model down. The three are distinguished by their histories, not their outputs, and the semantics keeps them apart. Creativity lives in construction. Selection produces the raw material construction works on. Declaration is permitted only as a modelling convenience, and a claim about creativity cannot rest on it.`
L14: BLANK
L15: `Questions have provenance too. A question — its target, its scope of admitted changes, what it asks — can be found as well as answered, and the semantics represents both. This is what distinguishes it from any class in which questions are inputs: such a class cannot represent question-finding, and question-finding is at least half of creativity.`
L16: BLANK
L17: `The **constitutive conjecture** is this: explanatory creativity is fully characterized by (i) organizations and the changes they admit, (ii) transports between organizations checked by change-fidelity alone, (iii) the provenance of those transports and of the questions they serve, and (iv) their physical realization. A genuine explanatory achievement that these four cannot represent counts against the conjecture. A candidate that satisfies all four while plainly explaining nothing counts against it too.`
L18: BLANK
L19: `## What this document does not claim`
L20: BLANK
L21: `It does not derive reference from uninterpreted matter. It derives reference from matter **plus a selection or construction history**. Physics says what organization a lump of matter instantiates; history says how that organization came to track another. Neither alone yields representation.`
L22: BLANK
L23: `It does not say prediction is explanation. Fidelity in this semantics is over **component structure under change**, not over outputs. Two systems with identical outputs and different internal routes are different organizations here, and the semantics says so.`
L24: BLANK
L25: `It does not supply an objective aesthetics, a probability of truth, a merit function, or a ranking of thinkers. It supplies places where such things would go if anyone had them, and marks those places as empty.`
L26: BLANK
L27: `It does not prove that any human, machine, institution, or lineage belongs to the classes defined. It defines the classes.`
L28: BLANK
L29: `## Grievances, anticipated`
L30: BLANK
L31: `**1. "Without declared kinds you cannot tell a cause from a correlation."**`
L32: `You can, and only this way. A correlation has no component that responds to an intervention on its supposed input; a cause does. The difference is a difference in edit-response, which is exactly what the semantics checks. Declared kind-labels added no discriminating power; where a separating change exists, edit-fidelity already finds it, and where none exists, the label was asserting a distinction the level does not contain.`
L33: BLANK
L34: `**2. "So this is operationalism: a thing is what you can do to it."**`
L35: `A *kind* is what a level's admitted changes can distinguish. A *content* is a whole organization, which can contain components that no change at that level separates — and then they are one kind *at that level*, which is correct rather than a loss. At a finer level with more admitted changes they may separate. The semantics indexes every kind-claim to its level. That is scoping, not operationalism.`
L36: BLANK
L37: `**3. "If correspondences are selected, you have made truth a matter of survival."**`
L38: `No. Selection produces a transport. Whether that transport is faithful on changes it was never selected against is a fact about the transport and the world, entirely independent of whether it survived. There is a theorem below (Derivation 3) that selected transports are *always* underdetermined on unseen changes. Survival is how the transport got there; fidelity is what it is.`
L39: BLANK
L40: `**4. "Then everything is relative to a contract of admitted changes, and there is no objectivity."**`
L41: `The physical theory fixes which changes are possible at all. A contract is a declared subset of those. An account is scoped to its contract and says so. Within any contract there is a fact of the matter about fidelity. A contract that quietly excludes physically possible changes to protect an account is not caught by a rule about intentions; it is caught by the requirement that the exclusion be stated, and then by any criticism that supplies the excluded change. Objectivity lives in the physics and in the fidelity facts; scope-honesty lives in the record.`
L42: BLANK
L43: `**5. "This is teleosemantics / structural realism / functionalism with new words."**`
L44: `It shares commitments with each and differs from each. From teleosemantics it takes the idea that a correspondence has a history; it differs by distinguishing selected from constructed histories and locating creativity only in the latter. From structural realism it takes structure-preservation; it differs by making preservation change-driven and component-level, and by refusing a global isomorphism requirement. From functionalism it takes substrate-independence; it differs by imposing physical realization obligations at every attribution. Whether the combination is new is a question about the literature, not about whether it is right.`
L45: BLANK
L46: `**6. "You have replaced explanation with evolution. That is deflationary."**`
L47: `Selection appears once, at the bottom, to produce the primitive layer of things-with-persistence that explanation operates on. Construction is a separate provenance with a separate witness, and every creative attribution requires it. Nothing about construction is reduced to selection; the semantics forbids that reduction in Part IV.`
L48: BLANK
L49: `**7. "Mathematics has no interventions. This cannot handle mathematical explanation."**`
L50: `An admitted change need not be a physical intervention. Removing an axiom, dropping a constraint, or changing a dimension are edits on an organization, and fidelity under them is well-defined. A proof explains, relative to a question, when its components respond to those edits as the target structure does. This is worked in Part VII.`
L51: BLANK
L52: `**8. "Where is aesthetics?"**`
L53: `In Part XI, as a declared normative relation. The semantics gives artistic effect, artistic purpose, and aesthetic reasons three distinct mathematical carriers and refuses to define any as another. It does not pretend to know which aesthetic reasons are true.`
L54: BLANK
L55: `**9. "'Selected' is just as much a stipulation as 'is a cause'. Who says the selection happened?"**`
L56: `Selection is a physical history: a population, a variation operator, a survival condition, and a sequence of events. Whether it occurred is a claim about the physical module, fallible but checkable in principle. A kind-label is not a claim about anything; it is a label. The difference is the difference between a hypothesis and a stipulation.`
L57: BLANK
L58: `**10. "Freezing the question for assessment while letting questions change across episodes is having it both ways."**`
L59: `It is having it both ways deliberately, and the two ways are indexed so they cannot be confused. An assessment is an event with a frozen contract. An episode is a history in which contracts can change, and every change carries a provenance record. Derivation 7 shows the two are consistent.`
L60: BLANK
L61: `**11. "Kinds obviously exist. A rule is not a cause."**`
L62: `A rule and a cause differ in how they respond to changes: a rule's application changes when the rule is edited and not when the world is intervened on; a cause's assignment changes under intervention. That is a difference in edit-signature, and the semantics represents it exactly. What the semantics denies is that the difference is available *before* the admitted changes are fixed, or independently of them.`
L63: BLANK
L64: `## Where to attack this`
L65: BLANK
L66: `The load-bearing claims, in order of how much falls if they fail:`
L67: BLANK
L68: `**(A) Sufficiency.** Produce a candidate that satisfies all four conditions of Account (Part V) on a physically admitted contract, with a non-declared transport, and that plainly explains nothing. This would show change-fidelity is not enough. The classic attempts — lookup tables, reversed calculations, conclusion-as-premise — all fail one of the four; a new one must fail none.`
L69: BLANK
L70: `**(B) Necessity.** Produce a genuine explanation whose organization cannot be captured by *any* transport satisfying component fidelity under *any* physically admitted contract. Explanations that work by showing a supposed structure is absent are the hard case; Part VII gives the semantics' treatment, and it may be inadequate.`
L71: BLANK
L72: `**(C) Reinstatement of kinds.** Produce a case in which a declared kind-label does discriminating work that no admitted change can do. Derivation 1 says this is impossible; a counterexample would reopen the question whether correspondence must be primitive after all.`
L73: BLANK
L74: `**(D) Genesis.** Show either that a selected transport can be non-underdetermined on unseen changes (against Derivation 3), or that construction reduces to selection (against Part IV), or that the primitive layer described in Part IV is not in fact what explanation operates on.`
L75: BLANK
L76: `**(E) Question-finding.** Show that treating a contract as a content — something that can be constructed, be new, and be the originative contribution of an episode — either trivializes creativity or fails to capture some genuine case of finding the right question.`
L77: BLANK
L78: `Anything else is a detail.`
L79: BLANK
L80: `# Part I — Commitments`
L81: BLANK
L82: `**Explanatory realism.** Whether a transport is faithful on a contract is independent of whether anyone accepts it. Systems can be wrong about their transports, their observations, their criticisms, and their own capacities.`
L83: BLANK
L84: `**Fallibility without falsehood-as-work.** A theory may contain an accurate scoped dependence together with errors elsewhere. What cannot count as explanation is an error in the very dependence alleged to do the work.`
L85: BLANK
L86: `**Conjecture, criticism, action.** An idea may be entertained without justification. A criticism has a target and is itself conjectural. A thinker may act on an appraisal without certifying it.`
L87: BLANK
L88: `**Recursive scrutiny with operative return.** Any aspect of a system's practice — a question, a contract, a transport, a method, an attention policy — can become a target, and the result must be able to change how the system proceeds.`
L89: BLANK
L90: `**Substrate independence with physical obligations.** Any carrier may bear an organization. Every attribution of an organization to a physical system must be permitted by the adopted physics.`
L91: BLANK
L92: `**Two provenances, not one.** Selection and construction both produce correspondences. They are distinguished by their histories. Neither is reduced to the other.`
L93: BLANK

Part V text:

"# Part V — Account

An explanatory candidate for question \(p\) is an organization \(E\), a transport \(t=(\pi,\tau,\sigma,\lambda)\) from \(D\) to \(E\), and an identified set \(\Gamma\) of active commitments in \(E\). The pair \(\mathcal E=(E,p,t,\Gamma)\) satisfies \(\operatorname{Account}(\mathcal E)\) exactly when the following four conditions hold, each a condition on supplied relations under the changes in \(C\).

**Component fidelity.** For every active component \(k\) of \(E\) with anchor subnetwork \(\lambda(k)\subseteq D\), and every \((a,b)\in C\), the relation obtained by imposing the constraints of \(\lambda(k)\) and projecting away its hidden ports equals the component relation of \(k\) under the translated edit:

\[proj_{V_k}[Sol_{\lambda(k)}(a,b)]=L_k(\tau(a),\sigma(b)). \tag{F1}\]

And the assembled organization agrees:

\[\pi[Sol_D(a,b)]=Sol_E(\tau(a),\sigma(b)),\quad \tau(1)=1,\quad \tau(a_2a_1)=\tau(a_2)\tau(a_1). \tag{F2}\]

(F1) prevents an assembled match from hiding a wrong decomposition. (F2) prevents a set of locally correct pieces from hiding a lost shared constraint. Together they are fidelity at every level the contract reaches. By (K), (F1) entails that every component of \(E\) has the signature of its anchor; there is no further condition about kinds to state.

**Question fidelity.** For every \((a,b)\in C\),

\[Ans_E(\tau(a),\sigma(b))=Ans_p(a,b). \tag{A}\]

The query \(\mathcal Q\) is held fixed; an account of a different query is not an account of this one.

**Non-circular dependence.** The answer follows by evaluating \(E\) under its independent boundary conditions. The target's answer does not appear, at the declared grain, as an unanalysed boundary input or as a component; moving an assertion from an input slot into a component named "law" does not discharge this. There exists \((a,b)\in C\) that removes or replaces a nonempty block of \(\Gamma\) while preserving the other boundary conditions, under which the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined in the claimed way.

**Non-vacuity.** \(Sol_D(1,b_0)\neq\varnothing\). The contract \(C\) is a declared subset of the physically admitted edits, and every physically admitted edit excluded from \(C\) is excluded by a stated scope, not silently. A contract consisting only of relabelings, or excluding every change under which the active commitments could matter to \(\mathcal Q\), does not satisfy non-circular dependence and is therefore not a contract on which an account can be claimed."

"## Expectation, surprise, violation

Let \(t\) be selected on history \(H\) with contract \(C\). For an edit–boundary pair \((a,b)\in C\) actually occurring:

- the **expectation** is \(\operatorname{Ans}_S(\tau(a),\sigma(b))\);

A **construction response** introduces a new organization or a new transport with a construction witness. Only the second can be originative under Part X."

# F1 analysis

The core claim: Derivation 2 asserts that two candidates both satisfying (F1), (F2), (A) on C have (a) pairwise-of-one-kind components and (b) coinciding answer profiles.

(b) is trivially true: both equal Ans_p.

(a): Is it true under stated assumptions? Derivation 1 says each component of E has the signature of its own anchor λ(k) ⊆ D. For two candidates E, E' with transports t, t' from the same D: component k of E has sig of λ(k); component k' of E' has sig of λ'(k'). For k and k' to be "of one kind", we need sig(λ(k)) = sig(λ'(k')) up to port translation. Nothing forces this: the anchors can be different subnetworks with different signatures.

Refutation fails.

So no, they're not of one kind on a contract with asymmetric displacement edits.

Since (F1) is per-component-with-anchor, and the swap maps anchors to anchors, yes t_1' is faithful whenever t_1 is (with the port translation adjusted). Hmm, is that right? t_1' = t_1 ∘ swap (or swap ∘ t_1). Faithfulness of t_1' requires proj Sol_{swap(λ(k))} = L_k(τ'(a),σ'(b)) with τ' = swap-conjugated... The construction works if the swap is an automorphism of the edit structure of E... but the edit structure of E is what τ induces. # F2 analysis: (T2)

The text (line 376): "With one-step discrepancy ε and an L-Lipschitz next-step map, e_n ≤ ε Σ_{k<n} L^k. (T2) Without a modulus, no accumulated bound follows. An exact question is not silently replaced by an approximate one."

The finding: three missing hypotheses: (h1) matching starts (e_0 = 0), (h2) whose Lipschitz constant (the represented map T), (h3) where ε holds (every state reached). Counterexamples 2a (starting error), 2b (target map Lipschitz but not represented map), 2c (ε only at initial state). Classification: "drafting omission, for which an erratum suffices."

Now — is this right under the theory's stated assumptions? The key question: what are (T2)'s "stated assumptions"? The sentence: "With one-step discrepancy ε and an L-Lipschitz next-step map, e_n ≤ ε Σ_{k<n} L^k." The terms "one-step discrepancy", "e_n", "next-step map" are undefined in the theory. The predecessor defines them (with e_0 = 0, "the represented next-step map is L-Lipschitz", "on a stated scope"). The theory is a condensation — the finding treats the predecessor as context ("they are not assumptions of the theory under audit"). So the theory's stated assumptions are only what its own text states. The text states: "one-step discrepancy ε", "an L-Lipschitz next-step map".Now, how to judge? The instructions say: "A refutation shows that a counter-instance breaks a stated assumption, that the claim as written is true of it, or that the finding misreads a sentence." So I need to determine whether the counter-instances break stated assumptions of (T2). Since (T2)'s assumptions are terse and undefined, the question is whether the natural reading of "one-step discrepancy ε" includes "at every step/state" (which would break 2c) and whether "e_n" implicitly starts from a matched state (breaking 2a), and whether "next-step map" means the target's or the represented's (2b).

Let me think about the defence arguments:

(i) Placement fixes e_n: (T2) sits after "Functional transport" ("If π∘S_a = T_a∘π for every generator a, the same holds for every admitted finite composition") and under "Approximate transport". The exact square compares πSz with Tπz at one state z; the n-step square is πS^n = T^nπ; its failure is e_n = d(πS^n z, T^n π z), and e_0 = d(πz, πz) = 0 "by definition". Hmm — is e_0 = 0 "by definition"? If e_n := d(πS^n z, T^n π z) for a single starting state z of D, then e_0 = d(πz, πz) = 0 ✓ (given π is a function). So on this reading, matching starts are built into the definition of e_n, and counter-example 2a's "represented run at y_0 = -1" is not of this form:2a compares TWO runs (πS^n z_0 vs y_n with y_0 = -1 ≠ πz_0). So 2a misreads e_n as a two-run discrepancy. Is that reading forced by the text? The text never defines e_n. The predecessor says "Writing e_n for the discrepancy after n steps gives e_{n+1} ≤ L e_n + ε. With e_0 = 0, e_n ≤ ε Σ..." — the predecessor's "With e_0 = 0" is a hypothesis (or the base case). Hmm.

The finding's reply: "None of the defence's readings is written in the theory. The symbol e_n is never defined... Part XV speaks of 'stated assumptions', and a reading that only placement suggests is not stated."

Hmm. This is a judgment call. Let me think about what "under their stated assumptions" means for Part XV's defeat entry: "A counterexample to the finite monotone theorem, (I2), (O1), (T2), (CT2), or Derivations 1–3 under their stated assumptions." So the defeater is a counterexample to (T2) under (T2)'s stated assumptions. - 2a: one-step discrepancy 0.1 at every state ✓ ("the one-step discrepancy is |S(w) − T(w)| = 0.1 at every w"); both maps 2-Lipschitz, so "an L-Lipschitz next-step map" ✓ (L=2). Hmm. But if e_n is a free variable ("the discrepancy after n steps"), then 2a's e_n qualifies. The text: "With one-step discrepancy ε and an L-Lipschitz next-step map, e_n ≤ ε Σ_{k<n} L^k" — e_n appears with a subscript n but no definition. In the predecessor: "Writing e_n for the discrepancy after n steps gives e_{n+1} ≤ L e_n + ε. With e_0 = 0..." — "the discrepancy after n steps" — between what and what? But the predecessor is NOT under audit ("The predecessor passages are context... they are not assumptions of the theory under audit"). So the theory's stated assumptions are the theory's own words: "one-step discrepancy ε and an L-Lipschitz next-step map". Hmm.

Hmm, but wait: if we say the theory's stated assumptions are literally "one-step discrepancy ε and an L-Lipschitz next-step map" without more, then (T2) is false as stated (counterexamples 2b, 2c, and 2a under the two-run reading). Refutation attempts:

**Against 2a (h1):** The defence says placement + the theory's uniform practice of evaluating E at the translated point (π line 204; expectation line 234; (F2) line 257; (A) line 265; functional transport line 366; relational transport) fixes e_n = d(πS^n z, T^n πz) with e_0 = 0. Hmm! So is 2a a counterexample "under the stated assumptions"? The stated assumptions don't define e_n at all. The finding exploits this. But the instructions for refutation say: "a refutation shows that a counter-instance breaks a stated assumption, that the claim as written is true of it, or that the finding misreads a sentence." Can I show the finding misreads? The finding reads e_n as a free "discrepancy after n steps" between two runs with independent starts. The theory's text doesn't define it; the placement suggests the square-failure reading. Is choosing the two-run reading a "misreading"? Hmm. The theory says "With one-step discrepancy ε" — "one-step discrepancy" between what? Presumably between πS and Tπ at a state (the one-step square failure). On this coherent reading, (T2) is TRUE (with L = Lip of the represented map T and ε bounding the square failure at all reached states — see below). Now judge the proposed repair:

"**Approximate transport.** With one-step discrepancy \(d(\pi Sz,T\pi z)\le\varepsilon\) at every state \(z\) of the stated scope and an \(L\)-Lipschitz represented next-step map \(T\), the discrepancy after \(n\) steps from one state, \(e_n=d(\pi S^nz,T^n\pi z)\) (so \(e_0=0\)), satisfies \(e_n\le\varepsilon\sum_{k<n}L^k\). (T2) Without a modulus, no accumulated bound follows. An exact question is not silently replaced by an approximate one."

Check correctness: e_{n+1} = d(πS^{n+1}z, T^{n+1}πz) ≤ d(πS S^n z, T π S^n z) + d(T π S^n z, T^{n+1} πz) ≤ ε + L d(πS^n z, T^n πz) = ε + L e_n. With e_0 = 0: e_n ≤ ε Σ_{k<n} L^k ✓.

# F3 analysis: Non-circular dependence

The condition (line 270):
- S1: "The answer follows by evaluating E under its independent boundary conditions."
- S2: "The target's answer does not appear, at the declared grain, as an unanalysed boundary input or as a component; moving an assertion from an input slot into a component named 'law' does not discharge this."
- S3: "There exists (a,b) ∈ C that removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ while preserving the other boundary conditions, under which the answer profile changes or ceases to be determined in the claimed way."

Finding's points:
1. (a) Missing τ,σ in S3: (a,b) ∈ C is a D-edit/boundary pair, but Γ lives in E; the pair reaches E only via τ, σ. Classified as a drafting slip that the text's conventions fill. — The finding itself concedes this "does not stand" as a misfire. So point (a) is a non-issue (the finding says so).
2. (b) Γ never typed: three readings pull three ways ((F1) "active component k of E" → commitments = components; S3's "the other boundary conditions" → the removed block = boundary conditions; Part VI "the commitments in W" vs "the named background" → input settings = background). Four readings R-τ-in, R-τ-mech, R-E, R-lit.
3. On R-τ-mech, verdicts flip for Part VII's production case, O33, O5.
4. S3 is not an anti-circularity test on any reading ("p because p" meets S3 when the answer can be removed); S2 does the anti-circularity work.
5. R-E alone breaks line 272 (relabeling contract): under R-E, deleting a working block leaves the answer undetermined at (1,b_0), so S3 holds on a relabeling-only contract — but line 272 says such a contract "does not satisfy non-circular dependence". So the "(a,b) ∈ C" in S3 is load-bearing.

Defence & replies: the defence says read charitably → R-τ-in is the only reading on which S3, line 272 and the theory's worked cases all agree. Reply: line 120 settles what interventions do, not whether the replaced component is in Γ; the theory doesn't choose; Part VI's wording invites R-τ-mech.

Proposed repair (option B, preferred): S3 becomes "There exist (a,b) ∈ C and a nonempty block G ⊆ Γ such that the answer profile at (a,b) differs from its value at (1,b_0), or is not determined there in the claimed way, and in E|(Γ∖G), with the named background fixed, that difference is lost or the answer ceases to be determined."

Option A (alternative): add τ,σ + type Γ as "components and boundary values of E, including the components that assign the inputs the candidate commits to"; note line 120.

My tasks: try to refute the finding; judge the repair (check Derivations 1, 8, 9, 10, Part VI, Part VII worked cases, "What (E) excludes/does not exclude", O33, O5).

Let me first try to refute.

S3: "removes or replaces a nonempty block of Γ while preserving the other boundary conditions" — hmm, "while preserving the other boundary conditions" — if Γ's members were boundary conditions, "the other boundary conditions" = those outside the block. Actually the phrase "while preserving the other boundary conditions" is natural if the removed block consists of boundary conditions/inputs; if the block is components, "the other boundary conditions" is a separate clause ("the edit preserves boundary conditions other than those in the block"?). **Derivation 1** ("Kind preservation is a theorem"): Claim: "If a transport satisfies (F1) on C, then every active component k of E has the same signature on τ[C] as its anchor λ(k) has on C, up to the port translation." The finding (F1) notes this in defence (iii): "(K) applies to subnetworks as well as components. Derivation 1's proof (line 555) itself takes the signature of a subnetwork projected onto a port set." Hmm — "up to the port translation": the anchor's projected relation lives on V_k via the translation ✓ fine.

Hmm, but one wrinkle: "every active component k of E has the same signature on τ[C]" — τ[C] = {(τ(a),σ(b))}? C⊆ A_D×B_D; τ[C] should be {(τ(a),σ(b)):(a,b) ∈ C} ✓ fine (though τ[C] alone drops σ — sloppy notation but fine).

Hmm wait — maybe the intended reading: the "transport" t encodes, for each (a,b), the transported relation — i.e., t's "value at (a,b)" = the relation it assigns at (a,b) (like L'_k(τ(a),σ(b))). And "alter L_j(a,b)" means alter the transported relation at (a,b) — i.e., alter t's data. Hmm: "The component relations L_j(a,b) are supplied independently for each (a,b). Survival on H constrains only {L_j(a,b) : (a,b) ∈ H}. Alter L_j(a,b) for one (a,b) ∉ H to any other admitted relation; the result survives on H and differs at (a,b)."

Hmm — in the semantics, a transport t = (π,τ,σ,λ) doesn't contain relations L_j. The relations L_j belong to the organizations. So "altering L_j(a,b)" alters an organization's interpretation. **Finite monotone theorem** (Part VI): "If Γ is finite, S is upward closed, and Γ ∈ S, then the critical singletons are exactly ∪min S and the globally indispensable ones are exactly ∩min S." Type (ii) — sufficiency: "A candidate that satisfies all four on a physically admitted contract with a non-declared transport and plainly explains nothing." The theory says the classic attempts (lookup tables, reversed calculations, conclusion-as-premise) fail one of the four. Can I find a new one? Let me think hard.

The four: (F1) component fidelity (each active component's relation = its anchor's projected relation at every (a,b) ∈ C); (F2) assembled agreement + τ homomorphism; (A) question fidelity; Non-circular dependence; Non-vacuity.
